#181. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#179. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#177. Lowe's posts strong online sales, raises outlook despite slow housing market

Lowe's on Wednesday posted strong quarterly results and raised its full-year outlook as the company said it saw strength among professional shoppers.

The company said comparable sales grew 1.8% for the fiscal third quarter, driven by a 17.4% increase in online sales and "solid" performance in pro and home services sales. Net sales rose to $22.76 billion.

Because of the strength, Lowe's now said it expects full-year sales of roughly $92.5 billion, up from a previous projection of $92 billion. It also expects comparable sales to be up 0.5%, compared with previous guidance of flat.

CEO Marvin Ellison told CNBC that the company is focused on growing despite economic headwinds and a sluggish housing market. He said Lowe's posted its sixth straight quarterly comparable sales increase and saw particular strength in appliances, which saw its largest revenue growth in five years.

"We believe irrespective of the macro environment we're in, we're going to take share because there's a lot of share out there that we can go and get, and we're going to be aggressive to go and get that share," Ellison told CNBC.

Lowe's also reported a pretax benefit of approximately $250 million from tariff refunds during the quarter, which contributed a 34-cent boost to earnings per share. Ellison said the company plans to use those funds to enhance the customer experience and bring value to shoppers.

He added that do-it-yourself customers and pro shoppers are not investing in major renovation projects but are instead spending on repair and maintenance projects.

"We are giving those pros what they need," he said. "We're able to improve our fulfillment capabilities and our pro loyalty program."

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 30, Lowe's reported net income of $1.67 billion, or $2.98 per share, compared with $1.62 billion, or $2.88 per share, the year prior.

The home improvement retailer has previously said it's seeing "pressure" on spending on projects, especially as consumers are stretched thin by inflation and macroeconomic uncertainty. The company is also battling a slower housing market and higher mortgage rates.

Ellison previously told CNBC he expects consumers to remain cautious through the end of the year.

Lowe's executives said on a call with analysts Wednesday that the company is continuing to battle high fuel costs, which they said accelerated through the third quarter. CFO Brandon Sink said diesel prices are currently around $6 per gallon, up about 15% overall compared to what the company saw in the second quarter.

"So we're trying to continue to manage that, balance that, and we're reflecting that into our Q4 guide," Sink said.

Ellison told CNBC the company remains optimistic about consumer spending this holiday season and that November sales are in line with expectations so far.

Lowe's competitor Home Depot said earlier this week that it's seeing resilience among professional shoppers and reported comparable sales growth.

#175. Europe plans to catch the next computer chip wave

Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data

"Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough," says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.

If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.

The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.

They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).

Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.

Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.

"With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago."

American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.

Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent "a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips".

"Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house'," says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.

"We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing."

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently.

"[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer," says van Mol.

His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.

It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.

"That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.

"In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it."

Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment

Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.

The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.

Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.

"The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip," says van Mol.

"You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology."

ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.

"Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing," says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.

Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a "launchpad" for European tech

ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.

The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.

In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as "a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy".

"By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction," he said.

Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.

In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called "the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.

The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.

It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.

Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.

If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.

"Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible", Teer says.

"To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key."

Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.

It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.

"What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product," Ton van Mol says.

Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.

It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.

Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.

For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.

"The demand is there", says van Mol, "and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

#174. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#173. Burberry shares rise on report Moncler could bid for it

#172. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#171. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#169. Target shares rise 7% as retailer posts best sales growth in five years, hikes guidance

Target on Wednesday reported its best sales growth in more than five years and beat Wall Street's revenue expectations, the latest signs of its turnaround strategy taking hold.

The retailer said its net sales grew 7.1% during the fiscal third quarter. Comparable sales rose 5.6%, compared with StreetAccount estimates of up 3.8%. That growth was boosted by a 5% increase in comparable traffic, Target added.

"[It's] a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often," CEO Michael Fiddelke told reporters.

Digital comparable sales jumped nearly 10% during the quarter.

Encouraged by the growth, Target raised its full-year guidance, upping its net sales growth guidance by 1 percentage point to around 6%. It's also expecting full-year operating income margin rate to be around 6.2%, which includes roughly 1.1 percentage points of benefit from tariff refunds.

The company also said it's now projecting full-year earnings per share to be in a range of $10.65 to $11.40, compared with prior guidance of between $9.90 and $10.90.

"The changes we've made across our assortment and guest experience are adding up," Fiddelke told reporters. "While we're encouraged by the progress, we know there's more work ahead to sustain this momentum and deliver consistent growth over time."

Target shares climbed about 7% in afternoon trading Wednesday.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, Target reported net income of $1.21 billion, or $2.65 per share, up from $689 million, or $1.51 per share, a year earlier.

This year, the company said it's invested approximately $3.8 billion in capital expenditures, up nearly $1 billion from last year. Because of those returns, Fiddelke said Target is now expecting to invest between $5 billion and $6 billion in capex next year.

On a call with analysts, he added that those investments will go toward more store remodels, technology, supply chain improvements, among other initiatives.

The company said it saw particular strength in beauty, which saw 9% growth, and food and beverage, where sales increased nearly 7%. The retailer added that net sales rose in all three months of the third quarter, with the strongest performance during the back-to-school season.

Fiddelke told reporters the quarter's strength showed "continued progress" toward Target's priorities that it outlined at the beginning of its turnaround.

As it looks to the holiday season, Target said it's marketing thousands of products priced at $5 and under, along with new artificial intelligence-powered shopping experiences to drive engagement. Fiddelke said Target is "encouraged" by shopping behaviors it's seen so far in the current quarter.

The big-box retailer has been showing signs of returning to growth, as it sees stronger sales trends after years of flat revenue. The results come after Target last quarter hiked its full fiscal-year outlook and said it saw "broad-based" strength across its various categories.

It's also lowered prices on more than 10,000 items in an effort to get customers back into its stores, especially as consumer sentiment lags and inflation remains high. The retailer said on Wednesday that it cut prices on an additional roughly 2,000 items during the third quarter.

Target's stock is up more than 70% this year.

#168. Tinned food and long-life milk boost retail sales after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Retail sales rose in October, driven by people buying long-life food such as tinned goods and UHT milk, according to official figures.

Sales volumes were 0.7% higher as people responded to the government's emergency preparedness "Be Ready" campaign, the Office for National Statistics (ONS) said.

The campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

Separate research released last week suggested that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days' worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

Data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator indicated last week that people were stocking up on items including tinned meat, bottled water and hand sanitiser.

The latest official sales figures, external from the ONS add to the evidence that the public has begun stockpiling.

"Supermarkets have told us that sales of long-life food items such as tinned food and UHT milk were boosted by the government's 'Be Ready' campaign," ONS chief economist Grant Fitzner said.

"Meanwhile fuel sales have been recovering from earlier low levels, despite pump prices rising again."

The rise in retail sales last month beat some economists' expectations - they had predicted a slight fall - and the increase came after a 0.6% decline in September.

As well as sales being boosted by people buying essentials, visits to art galleries and clothing purchases were also higher in October.

The boost in sales comes at the beginning of the festive period, which is normally the busiest time of the year for retailers.

Jacqui Baker, partner and head of retail at auditing firm RSM UK, said that "retailers will want to see out the year with some Christmas cheer before consumers batten down the hatches in the New Year as energy costs skyrocket and inflation ramps up further".

Typical energy bills are forecast to go up by Â£443 per year from January, pushing up the pace of price rises.

Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Why should I stockpile and what will I need?

Separate economic data released on Friday showed the government borrowed slightly less than economists had expected in October.

Borrowing - the difference between public sector spending and income - was Â£17bn last month, the ONS said., external

Although that was Â£0.8bn higher than October last year, it was below analysts' forecasts of about Â£18bn.

Interest repayments were Â£11.2bn - the highest October debt interest figure on record - and Â£2.8bn more than a year ago.

These repayments have been pushed up by higher inflation, as payments on index-linked government bonds are linked to the inflation rate.

Inflation rose to 3.6% in October, mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices. The rate has been rising in recent months as the Iran war has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

#162. GTA 6 sales hit $3.8bn in a single day - the biggest entertainment launch in history

Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6) saw record retail sales in its first full-day of release, hitting $3.8bn (Â£2.9bn), making it the biggest entertainment launch in history.

Take-Two, the parent company of Rockstar Games, said on Friday the figures were based on estimates through end of day on Thursday and marked a record for "entertainment across any medium". It pre-sold 32.5m copies of the game.

To compare, Spider-Man: Brand New Day, this year's highest-grossing film so far, made $2.5bn (Â£1.8bn) over its entire run in cinemas.

Internet infrastructure firm Cloudflare said earlier that PS5 downloads in the UK peaked at 8.6 times normal levels around its launch.

New Zealand, where GTA 6 was first available, saw a 14.8-fold spike four hours before, suggesting some players switched regions for early access.

In its initial findings since the game's launch, Cloudflare also recorded a huge surge in traffic to Rockstar's online services, peaking at 11.2 times normal levels in Australia and 9.3 times on the US East Coast.

In Europe, UK interest was particularly strong, reaching seven times normal levels compared with 5.3 times in France and Germany.

The record smashes that of its predecessor - from 13 years prior - Grand Theft Auto V. The game previously held the best opening week sales in video-game history, earning $1.15bn (Â£1.2bn) in just five days.

The game is the latest instalment in Rockstar's nearly three decade old crime simulator franchise, which has become one of the most popular games in history.

At its core, the series is a satirical take on the American dream, putting players in the shoes of career criminals in Leonida, the game's Florida-inspired state.

The latest release follows new characters, a pair of small-time criminals Lucia and her partner Jason.

GTA 6 is estimated to have cost more than $1bn to develop, which would make it the most expensive video game in history.

BBC News Mundoâs Rafael Rojas visits the real-life Miami locations that inspired GTA 6's Vice City.

Research firm Sensor Tower said GTA 6 will be so popular that it will reduce the time gamers spend on other single-player titles by 10%.

The firm said its estimates are based on email receipts data and other statistical sources that track millions of people daily. They also track public profiles across major gaming platforms like PlayStation and Xbox, where GTA 6 is available.

Around three quarters of the game's sales have been on PlayStation 5 and the balance on Xbox, Sensor Tower said.

Niko Partners, a gaming market research firm, said it expects GTA 6 to make more than $3bn in sales worldwide this year.

Witness History: The launch of Grand Theft Auto

That figure would rank GTA 6 among the highest-grossing entertainment releases ever, but it would still have some way to go to catch 1939's Gone with the Wind, which has made an estimated $4.5bn (Â£3.44bn), adjusted for inflation.

Rockstar is expected to make even more money from GTA 6 over the coming years.

The New York-based studio has yet to announce when the game will be released for PCs or given a date for its multiplayer expansion, which will allow players to interact and go on missions with others over the internet.

If 2013's GTA 5 is any guide, multiplayer will add dozens more hours of gameplay and bring in even more revenue for Rockstar as players spend real money on in-game property, cars and other perks.

GTA 6 launched around the world on 19 November

Anticipation for the blockbuster title has grown through repeated delays, following a 13-year wait.

GTA 6 has been hailed by some reviewers as one of the most impressive gaming experiences ever. Entertainment media site IGN described it as an "uncompromisingly engineered" masterpiece.

The release of GTA 6 at midnight on Thursday across various time zones saw many gamers stay up through the night, while some took the following day off to play.

The launch broke Virgin Media O2's evening traffic record as people rushed to install GTA 6's huge 212GB file on consoles.

The game's graphic content has resulted in censorship in Japan and, like earlier GTA releases, is not approved for sale in mainland China.

Police departments have also reminded Americans that street racing, auto theft and gun violence are still crimes in real life, as excitement reached a fever pitch in the US.

#160. Hims & Hers launches new peptide treatments — while admitting there's more to learn about efficacy

Hims & Hers Health on Wednesday said it will offer five formulations of peptides and longevity treatments compounded in the U.S. as part of the initial rollout of its Peptides+ platform, all of which are not approved by regulators.

Even as it announced the new products, Hims & Hers acknowledged it has more to learn about how best to use the peptides and how effective they are — underscoring the uncertainty surrounding the growing space.

The new offerings come after the federal government eased restrictions on compounding certain unapproved peptides, which are short chains of amino ​acids. Those products have exploded in popularity among fitness and longevity enthusiasts who attest that they help with muscle growth, rapid injury recovery and anti-aging, even though most lack evidence from robust human trials.

Hims in late October launched an early access list for the upcoming peptide offerings, and more than 10,000 people signed up within 15 days, a company spokesperson said in a statement.

None of the company's specific compounded products are approved by the Food and Drug Administration. But Hims said they are authorized by law and formulated by pharmacies "that adhere to rigorous quality standards and are licensed and inspected by state boards of pharmacy."

The products include compounded formulations of sermorelin, a peptide that stimulates growth-hormone release and is marketed for recovery, body composition and anti-aging; glutathione, a peptide promoted ‌for anti-aging and athletic recovery; and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide, or NAD+, a coenzyme that plays a role in cellular metabolism.

The company will also offer compounded versions of vitamin B-12, a vitamin needed to make red blood cells, build DNA, and maintain healthy nerve function, and L-carnitine, an amino acid derivative that helps the body turn fat into energy. The FDA has approved certain prescription forms of both peptides for established medical uses, primarily for diagnosed deficiencies in those compounds.

Hims said it plans to add more peptides and therapies made in the U.S. over time. That includes BPC-157, an unapproved peptide derived from a protein found in human gastric juice that is marketed for healing processes. The company also aims to introduce another unapproved peptide called TB-500, which is derived from a naturally occurring protein in the body and is marketed for broader, systemic soft-tissue recovery and flexibility. Most of the research on both has been conducted in animals, not humans.

To receive the products, members on the Hims platform will start with an assessment of health history and goals with a licensed provider trained in peptide therapies, who can tailor a personalized plan to their needs, if appropriate.

The company said it intends to use its communication platform to check in with patients and ensure they are seeing desired results.

"If they're not, we need to do something about it," Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, Hims chief medical officer, told CNBC. "We need to, you know, adjust the dose when that's appropriate, or in some cases consider other compounds that's maybe a better fit. And of course, there will also be cases when we need to refer people to other care options."

However, Vinjamoori said there's still uncertainty over how to best use the peptides.

"We don't have all of this data that we're used to having with compounds like this," he said. "We have the opportunity to get it right, and I think we see that as our responsibility with a company of our size, our skill, and resources to really lead."

Vinjamoori added that people who have serious illnesses, such as cardiovascular disease, liver disease and kidney disease, will not be candidates for the compounds for now.

Meanwhile, Andrew Huberman, a well known neuroscientist, podcaster and Stanford University School of Medicine professor who recently joined Hims' board of directors, in a statement called compounds "real medical treatments, not just a wellness trend." He said Hims provides safe access through "trusted sourcing and physician oversight."

But health experts raised concerns about the broader mainstream marketing of peptides in the U.S. and accessing them on telehealth platforms.

"With peptides, I think you have to view them the same way you would view any drug that hasn't been rigorously tested," said Joshua Kritzer, a chemistry professor at Tufts University. "Without actual testing, all you have is anecdotal information, and that's just not enough information to put a substance in your body."

Kritzer said the lack of large clinical trials means researchers can't rule out potentially serious, rare complications from peptides — the kinds of risks that have led regulators to reject or pull otherwise effective drugs from the market after they underwent comprehensive testing in humans.

Without that testing, it's also unclear how telehealth firms, physicians and pharmacies can make decisions about what doses patients should take, said Paul Knoepfler, a cell biology and human anatomy professor at UC Davis Health. While some people rely on anecdotal data from real peptide users, "the dosing for these products really has not been established," he added.

Knoepfler said one risk is that injecting too much of a synthetic peptide into patients could trigger "weird immune responses that are really bad for one's health" because the body might recognize it as foreign.

"With the peptides, it's very loosey-goosey," Knoepfler told CNBC. "How does one even know what would be the effective amount for whatever you're hoping it will do, and what amount would have more risks?"

But he said the more contact patients can have with physicians around taking a compounded peptide, the better. Providers, including those tied to telehealth firms, should make sure patients are well-informed about the possible risks and unknowns of a given product and consider their preexisting conditions, cancer history and other medications, Knoepfler said.

That appears to be consistent with the approach Hims & Hers is taking. The company says patients who use the peptide and longevity products can also use its Care+ offering, which pairs treatment with ongoing support. That includes twice-a-year lab testing to track their progress and the ability to message providers trained in peptide therapy around the clock with any questions.

Still, Kritzer argued that having a doctor who only knows a patient from online, essentially mail-order platforms or websites "is not enough supervision to put you on a prescription medication," especially an unapproved one.

Hims' ability to legally sell some of these peptides stems from a major shift in federal oversight by the Health and Human Services Department. It issued a policy in October that says the Food and Drug Administration will not take enforcement action against licensed pharmacies, federal facilities or physicians that compound and dispense six unapproved peptides while formal rules are reviewed.

In practice, that means these pharmacies and other firms can now offer them, even though the peptides aren't yet on the FDA's official list of ingredients compounding pharmacies are allowed to make. It does not translate to formal approval of the specific peptides.

The policy followed a 2023 decision by the Biden administration where the FDA restricted compounding pharmacies from producing popular peptide formulations due to safety, impurity and immune-reaction concerns.

HHS Secretary ​Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has supported broader access to peptides, calling himself a "big fan" of the treatments and arguing that restrictions were driving Americans to potentially unsafe products from overseas. Kennedy has previously described using BPC-157 to treat a muscle injury.

The announcement also comes after Hims in February 2025 acquired a California-based peptide manufacturing facility equipped with advanced peptide synthesizers.

The evidence for peptide efficacy varies by compound.

A 2026 systematic review screening 113 studies, including both small trials on humans along with rodent research, found zero eligible clinical outcome trials proving that administering IV or intramuscular NAD+ improves anti-aging, energy, or wellness endpoints in humans.

There is some evidence for specific uses of certain peptides, such as potential weight loss. A review of 37 L-carnitine trials found participants lost about 2.7 pounds on average, though that analysis does not demonstrate that a compounded injection works the same way.

Meanwhile, vitamin B-12 injections are most commonly used to help people with a diagnosed deficiency rather than preventative care. U.S. survey data shows only 3.6% of adults are B-12 deficient.

Glutathione's evidence is thin, too. IV injections of it are widely marketed for skin whitening, yet not FDA-approved for this purpose.

A small study published in the Journal of Pakistan Association of Dermatologists evaluating IV glutathione showed that the treatment is "not very effective" for skin-lightening, and that it loses efficacy with time.

The FDA also said in August that it received reports of at least 30 patients who experienced adverse events after they received compounded IV glutathione alone or in combination with other ingredients from different compounding pharmacies.

All of the glutathione tied to the reports were from the same wholesale distributor and was labeled as dietary supplement grade, which is not an appropriate ingredient to make injectable drugs. Two of the Texas pharmacies conducted recalls related to elevated endotoxin levels in their glutathione injectable drugs.

These studies and reports don't speak to how Hims' exact combination will perform, but they do highlight the current limits on data studying the treatments and the questions surrounding them.

#157. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#154. Social Security benefits are rising in 2027, but other changes could affect what you keep

Whether you're working or retired, changes to Social Security in 2027 could affect your finances.

Starting in January, millions of program beneficiaries will get a 3.6% increase to their benefits, thanks to the annual cost-of-living adjustment. But rising Medicare premiums could chip away at the increase — and as beneficiaries' incomes rise, that may affect the taxes they owe and their eligibility for a new senior deduction.

Meanwhile, a higher taxable earnings cap means some workers will pay Social Security taxes on more income, reducing their paycheck.

Here's a breakdown of what to expect in the new year.

Social Security's limit for payroll taxes is adjusted every year to reflect the average wage index.

In 2027, the taxable maximum will go up to $192,300 in earnings, up from $184,500 in 2026. Workers and employers each pay a 6.2% payroll tax on earnings up to that limit — or 12.4% for self-employed workers. Medicare taxes of 1.45% for both workers and employers are not subject to a limit.

In 2027, workers who earn $192,300 or more will pay $11,922.60 in total Social Security payroll taxes, according to the Social Security Administration, while self-employed workers will pay $23,845.20.

"If you're somebody whose income is over what the new threshold is going to be, this increase in the threshold results in a tax increase to you," said Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird.

The 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment for 2027 is slated to increase retirement benefits by about $75 per month on average, according to the Social Security Administration.

The annual adjustment is the largest boost to benefits since 2023 due to rising inflation. The COLA averaged 2.6% from 2000 to 2026, according to the Senior Citizens League, a nonpartisan senior group.

Around 72 million Social Security beneficiaries will see benefit increases, according to the agency.

The maximum benefit for a worker at full retirement age — when individuals become eligible for 100% of the benefits they've earned — will rise to $4,188 per month in 2027, up from $4,152 per month in 2026, according to the federal agency. To be eligible for the maximum benefit at full retirement age — typically age 66 to 67 — you generally have to earn up to the taxable threshold for each of your working years factored into the calculation.

How much of the COLA increase Social Security beneficiaries see depends in part on their Medicare premiums, which may be deducted directly from benefit checks.

Monthly rates are poised to increase for both Medicare Part B, which covers medically necessary services and preventive care, and Part D, which provides prescription drug coverage.

The standard monthly Medicare Part B premium will be $209.40 in 2027 — a $6.50 increase from $202.90 in 2026, according to the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

"It's not often that we see a Part B [premium] increase that is lower than the COLA," Mary Johnson, an independent Social Security and Medicare policy analyst, said of the 3.2% increase. The last time that happened was in 2023, she said.

Medicare Part D beneficiaries may also pay higher premiums in 2027.

Assuming enrollees keep the same plan, the average enrollment-weighted monthly Part D premium may be $51 in 2027, up 50% from $34 in 2026, according to projections from KFF, a provider of health policy research and polling. That is higher than CMS' projections, which forecast the national average premium for standalone Part D plans will rise about $1, to $36, in 2027 from $35 in 2026.

Standard monthly premiums for Part B and plan-specific premiums for Part D apply to individuals who have $112,000 or less in modified adjusted gross income, or $224,000 for married couples. Beneficiaries with higher income pay larger premiums because of income-related monthly adjustment amounts, which affect 9% of Medicare Part B and Part D beneficiaries, according to CMS.

With bigger Social Security benefits, more households may find that some portion of benefits is taxable — though a new senior deduction may help provide some relief to those who are eligible.

Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income taxes for certain individuals. The thresholds at which those taxes are applied are not adjusted from year to year.

Whether or not benefits are taxed is determined by a formula known as combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest income and half of Social Security benefits:

Individuals who are subject to taxes may opt to withhold taxes from their Social Security benefit checks, rather than face a potential bill during tax filing season.

Separately, a temporary senior deduction of up to $6,000 for individuals ages 65 and over may help to indirectly reduce taxes on Social Security benefits.

That deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in 2025, helps reduce retirees' taxable income, according to Steffen. It's in place through 2028. Because the break phases out for individuals with modified adjusted gross income starting at $75,000 — and for married couples with $150,000 — it's important to watch your income if you're close to those limits, Steffen said.

"Keep in mind that if you're over 65, you might qualify for this deduction," Steffen said. "But if you let your income get too big, you might lose the deduction."

Moreover, any extra income that causes you to lose the deduction may also come with its own taxes, making it more expensive, according to Steffen.

"What we've been telling our clients is that tax planning is a lot more complicated than it used to be, and you can't do it on the back of an envelope anymore," he said. "You need to use good tools that can do good calculations for you."

Individuals who claim Social Security retirement benefits before their full retirement age and who continue to work may be subject to withholdings.

In 2027, individuals under full retirement age may earn up to $25,560 per year, up from $24,480 in 2026, without adjustments to their Social Security benefits. For every $2 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits will be withheld.

Importantly, those withholdings are not lost completely. Once a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, the withheld money will be credited to their benefits over their remaining lifespan.

In the year individuals reach full retirement age, the retirement earnings test limit is higher — $67,920 per year in 2027, up from $65,160 per year in 2026. For every $3 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits is withheld.

Starting the month a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, no earnings are withheld.

#152. UK to explore even closer ties with EU after agreeing deal, says Burnham

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen, Prime Minister Andy Burnham and European Council President Antonio Costa join hands at the end of a press conference during the EU-UK summit in Brussels

Andy Burnham said the UK will discuss possible options for even closer ties with the EU, after agreeing a deal covering British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

The prime minister said it was a "good day" for Britain and Europe, adding the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

European Council President AntÃ³nio Costa said the EU remains "open to have the closest possible relationship" with the UK.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal "isn't fair" - and Reform's Nigel Farage called it a "betrayal of the British people".

Legislation to implement the deal is expected to be introduced in the UK Parliament next week.

Burnham arrived in Brussels to finalise the latest stage of the government's post-Brexit "reset" of relations with the EU, which originally began under former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer.

The agreements announced on Friday included scrapping most of the checks on British food exports, in return for the UK following some EU regulations, from 2028.

The UK government believes the changes will cut costs for businesses and could bring prices down for shoppers, while critics argue the change will make the UK a "rule taker" from Brussels rather than a rule maker.

Watch: What the new UK-EU deal could mean for European relations

The new youth experience scheme will allow up to 100,000 18 to 31 year olds from the EU to travel, work and study in the UK for up to three years. The same number of young Britons will be allowed into Europe.

The two sides want to ensure that opportunities under the annual scheme will be available to young people by no later than January 2030.

A further six months is available to discuss a deal on tuition fees, which could prove to be a potential stumbling block.

There have been calls from the European side for fees to be reduced for EU students at British universities. The joint statement says any agreement would also be for UK students in the EU.

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she is "confident" a solution will be found, with Burnham adding: "Me too."

On immigration, the UK and EU have committed to working closely to tackle migrant smuggling and human trafficking. They also expect their law enforcement agencies to share more data.

Burnham said "significant progress" has been made to help "disrupt the gangs who are making those dangerous crossings across the English Channel".

The UK-EU statement also said talks on linking the EU and UK emissions trading systems have concluded and they aim to apply the agreement from 2028.

Watch: Will the UK-EU summit deal boost the economy?

Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

He added: "Beyond that we do need to consider what is the right long-term relationship. Incremental steps only take us so far."

The prime minister said his government will publish a 10-year plan next month, adding it will set out its ambitions for reindustrialisation, growth in "all parts of Britain" and opportunities for young people.

Burnham said these ambitions are "connected to our long-term relationship with our nearest partner".

He said over the coming months before the next UK-EU summit both sides will "seek to conduct an informal brainstorming process" to look at what those options are.

Burnham added: "What we are announcing today is probably the limit of what we can achieve in this Parliament, given what needs to be done to implement it.

"But we want to inform the Parliament after this one, so that decisions can be taken about that long-term relationship."

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The prime minister said the "British people will be the boss of what happens next", with the next election needing to be held by August 2029.

Von der Leyen, who said the summit was a "turning point" for the EU-UK relationship, said "we are open for brainstorming".

Badenoch said the deal was not fair because UK farmers would now have to follow rules made by Brussels and more young people will come to the UK to "compete with our young people who are struggling to get jobs".

Badenoch was also asked about the Treasury's analysis, which Burnham said suggested the deal "will add up to 0.5%" to UK gross domestic product and he described it as a "Â£17bn boost to the British economy".

She claimed Burnham has "plucked that figure out of the air" and "the only modelling I've seen that even talks about that figure says it's actually 0.0%".

Farage said Friday was a "very good day for the EU", adding: "A good day to try and humiliate the one member state that actually had the courage to say, 'We don't need to be part of you, we can govern our own country'."

In a social media post, Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, added: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud. Only Reform will stop the endless betrayal of the British people."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."
