#148. Ford starts U.S. battery cell production using Chinese tech amid geopolitical tensions

MARSHALL, Mich. — Ford Motor has started producing battery cells in the U.S. for its next generation of electric vehicles using technologies licensed by Chinese battery giant CATL.

The automaker said Wednesday that cell production at a $3 billion plant in Marshall, Michigan — roughly 100 miles west of Detroit — started last month and the cells are now shipping out. They'll be used in prototypes for its Fathom small electric pickup truck, which CEO Jim Farley has touted as rivaling Tesla and Chinese competitors.

The production marks a milestone for the Detroit automaker and is the next step toward using lower-cost lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, battery cells in U.S. vehicles. The technology was created in the U.S. but commercialized largely in China.

The automaker's decision to make the batteries at the roughly 2 million-square-foot facility has been criticized by some lawmakers and U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over Ford's ties to CATL.

Alan Clarke, Ford's vice president of advanced development projects, said licensing the technology from CATL helped the automaker bring the low-cost battery chemistry to the U.S. as quickly as possible.

"If we want to get to being able to produce affordable EVs in the United States, we need to also do it the fastest way possible," Clarke said Tuesday during a tour of the facility. "CATL being the biggest in the world means that they're the best supplier to get us that technology and help us learn ourselves how to produce LFP cells."

The U.S. government has increased scrutiny of Chinese ties with U.S. companies even as President Donald Trump is meeting this week with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

In September, Duffy sent a letter to Farley expressing "profound concern" about Ford's ties to Chinese companies that it believes could be detrimental to the Detroit carmaker and the U.S. automotive industry. The White House a day later followed up by commending the automaker for its domestic jobs and manufacturing.

Ford — the largest producer of U.S. vehicles — has routinely touted its American roots, jobs and manufacturing, especially as the Biden and Trump administrations have pushed to onshore and grow U.S. production.

Ford's battery plant in Marshall is expected to be capable of producing 40 battery cells a minute, equating to 20 gigawatt-hours annually for EVs and the company's energy storage business. That's down from initial plans of up to 40 gigawatt-hours annually amid an EV pullback by the automaker and broader industry.

Ford has said the production pace will help reduce battery costs and that the facility has room for additional capacity if needed.

The facility is highly automated, but Ford said it's still in the early stages of certifying its processes, as it plans to ramp up cell production next year before the expected launch of the Fathom pickup during the fourth quarter of 2027.

#147. MP criticises Guernsey and Jersey company ownership register plans

MP Sir Andrew Mitchell has campaigned for publicly accessible registers of who owns companies in British Overseas Territories and Crown Dependencies

Plans to improve transparency over company ownership in Guernsey and Jersey have come under criticism from a Westminster MP, who says they "do not go nearly far enough".

The comments come after Guernsey, external and Jersey, external set out plans to allow approved applicants access to information about who owns or controls companies when investigating suspected financial crime.

Sir Andrew Mitchell, who has campaigned for greater scrutiny of company ownership, said there were "immediate indications" the proposals would fall short.

He said limits on access to the information could mean "national security threats may remain entirely invisible to investigators".

The plans follow a joint commitment made by Guernsey, Jersey and the Isle of Man in December 2023 to increase transparency around company ownership and help tackle money laundering, terrorist financing and other financial crime.

Currently, information about who ultimately owns or controls companies is generally only available to law enforcement agencies, regulators and certain regulated businesses carrying out anti-money laundering checks.

The new proposals would allow journalists, academics and other applicants with a legitimate interest to apply for access.

Sir Andrew said investigators could face a "catch-22" because they may need access to the register to prove wrongdoing in the first place.

He also said he understood applicants would have to show they had been unable to find the information elsewhere, arguing: "This entirely defeats the object of a register."

Jersey's consultation response says applicants would be expected to show that "all reasonable alternative sources have been exhausted" before access was granted.

Guernsey's published response does not explicitly set out the same requirement.

He said: "Access should be open to those from other jurisdictions, for example those in the global south from whom much of the world's dirty money was stolen in the first place."

Sir Andrew also questioned whether users would have to pay to access the information, saying: "This information should be free at the point of access."

He said the proposals should eventually lead to fully public registers.

Both the governments of Guernsey and Jersey rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and said they continue to meet international standards in tackling financial crime.

A Government of Jersey spokesperson said the concerns raised by him "do not accurately reflect what is in Jersey's consultation response paper".

They said the policy was intended to help Jersey meet its international commitments while maintaining "a fair, proportionate, and effective transparency framework".

They added: "Jersey continues to meet international standards for transparency and countering money laundering, terrorist financing and proliferation financing."

Deputy Andrew Niles, from Guernsey's Policy & Resources Committee, rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and pointed to a positive 2025 assessment of the island's efforts to tackle financial crime, external.

He said: "Guernsey does not accept the suggestion that our proposals fall short of the commitments we've made on beneficial ownership transparency or that the island does not meet international standards on beneficial ownership transparency."

"The proposal is based on a tried and tested model adopted in the EU," he added.

#144. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#143. Tech stock sell-off drags markets down as Anthropic shares slide

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off dragged markets down.

The sell-off comes as concerns remain over the speed and size of artificial intelligence investment, but also as some large companies including Nvidia and SpaceX faced specific issues that worried investors.

Those pressures combined to weigh on US markets through Friday, with all key US indexes closing sharply down on the day, furthering earlier falls over the week.

Both the S&P 500 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average were pushed into correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The S&P 500 closed down 3.7%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.3%. However, all three indexes were up on where they were a year ago.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Investors were also spooked after the Democrats' resounding win in the US midterms in early November, which sparked fears of a reduction in AI development.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping at one point by more than 12% to about $85, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83. It closed down 8.5%, reversing half of the gains made on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50 before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday as the market sell-off continued.

Nvidia shares had plummeted by 13.7% by the close of trade, following Reuters reports - unconfirmed by the BBC - the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its accounting practices.

Musk's firms Tesla and SpaceX both experienced falls of more than 7.6%. SpaceX shares were hit after it announced a bond sale to finance what the company said was the largest purchase of Nvidia chips on record.

Meta closed down more than 5% and Amazon was down more than 4%, while Apple and Alphabet experienced smaller falls.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

#140. Investigators make progress in hunt for cause of FAA ground stop

Engineers and analysts searching for the root cause of the technical issues behind Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration have been able to replicate the situation that was overwhelming parts of the air traffic system, people familiar with the investigation told CNBC.

While replicating the issue in a lab in Florida is progress, those investigating the matter have yet to determine exactly why two routers in question failed, according to the people, who requested anonymity to talk about an ongoing situation. Finding the root cause is critical to the FAA determining if there may be an issue with other routers that form the backbone of communications for the national air transportation network.

The ground stop lasted for more than four hours Friday, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford have said there is no indication the routers failed due to a cyberattack.

The incident in question happened shortly after L3Harris Technologies, the primary contractor supporting the FAA telecommunications network, installed a new router at a facility in El Dorado, Arkansas.

L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

Investigators said within eight minutes of its installation, the router began to transmit too much data within the FAA network.

That surge of data, 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds, destabilized aircraft communications and radar feeds, Duffy said.

Since the ground stop, Duffy has increased the amount of money he wants to upgrade the national air traffic system. After originally asking for $30 billion to build a digital network that will incorporate artificial intelligence, Duffy has now asked Congress for $50 billion.

#138. Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

The prime minister made a lot of the UK's new deal with the EU cutting costs for business and consumers during tough times.

In another world, Andy Burnham would have loved for the nation's supermarkets to clearly announce that they could cut food prices as a result of this latest agreement on food exports.

And while grocers have very much welcomed Friday's announcement, they have notably not committed to price cuts as a direct result of this move.

The managing director of M&S, Alex Freudmann, called the deal a "major step forward", saying it would remove bureaucracy. Tesco chief executive Ken Murphy added it was "good news for British farmers and producers".

Freudmann highlighted some of hoops that were hurting UK-EU trade, using the example that "getting our spaghetti Bolognese from Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet's signature and even the Latin name of a cow".

Such measures will no longer need to be taken once this deal kicks in. Good news for business exports then, but will that lead to lower shopping bills here in the UK?

This deal will matter for food exporters - it may not have a clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders say there are too many moving parts in the world economy, especially given the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

It is more a hope than will at this stage. The industry and government hope that the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers.

This is not the first time Labour has sought to reduce the weekly shop costs. Sir Keir Starmer's administration floated the idea that supermarkets might even offer some form of branded discounts, a suggestion that was met with collective bemusement by those in the industry.

However, there will be less paperwork, checks and red tape on food imports too, but they were already lightly enforced.

No longer will hundreds of pages of documents be required. No longer will perishable goods lose valuable hours or days at border checks. No longer will the incorrect colour of ink or Latin name for a meat disallow a shipment of British exports.

Some, though not all of the 24% hit since 2019 to the real value of agrifood exports, could be regained by a bonfire of this red tape.

In particular, smaller exporters of food will be able to return to the large EU market.

But the extra benefit here is unclear, and there could be extra checks and costs required instead on non-EU food imports.

The government's own assessment is that the economy will gain Â£5bn through this, or 0.14% by 2040.

Critics point out that this benefit does not include the quid pro quo - having to align with EU laws. Farmers have pointed to compliance with pesticides and fungicide regulation.

The classic example of the benefits of post-Brexit autonomy has been the development of precision-bred foodstuffs, such as Vitamin D-enhanced tomatoes.

The producer of this technology has, however, backed the deal after the government negotiated an "exception period" for ongoing UK sale.

Professor Cristobal Uauy of the John Innes Centre said that "aligning with the EU market will bring our science to a greater number of people".

Bringing all the agreements on food, emissions and the youth experience together, the economy is forecast to get an overall boost of 0.5%, or Â£17bn a year.

A modest boost, but high by the standards of past post-Brexit trade deals, for example, with Australia, India or Japan and the Gulf and the trans-pacific deal.

This UK-EU pact actually matches all of those agreements combined for expected growth measured by the Treasury's own assessments.

The biggest single boost to the overall economy would come from more European young workers. However, the economy benefit of this part of the deal per person reduces to zero, according to the government's own calculations.

Burham also said he hoped a planned electricity deal with the EU would cut bills, but that has not been done yet, and requires a contribution into the bloc's budget for poorer regions.

The electricity deal could help lower bills, but that contains a clear trade-off.

There are still some details to be revealed in the full legal texts of these documents.

The bigger point is whether this logic of gaining free-flowing access for food, in exchange for giving up regulatory freedom, will now apply elsewhere.

Is food now a blueprint for future negotiations? It had been the Starmer-Rachel Reeves strategy to spread this model to other sectors such as cars, and chemicals.

The big takeaway from today was that both the PM and his counterparts publicly acknowledged the end of the incremental piecemeal approach to aligning with the single market.

The question is whether the PM thinks what goes for food applies across the whole economy. Most economists predict that could lead to much bigger economic gains, but it will also be a much bigger constitutional battle.

#136. Italian jeweller and businessman Paolo Bulgari dies aged 89

Italian jeweller Paolo Bulgari, who helped turn his family's business into a global luxury brand, has died in Rome aged age 89.

His family announced the news, remembering him as "the greatest jeweller of our time", according to Italian media.

Paolo Bulgari was Chairman of the Bvlgari Group since 1984 and its jewellery pieces have adorned Hollywood stars and royalty including Diana, Princess of Wales.

The company Bvlgari posted an online tribute to the "true creative soul", whose work would "continue to inspire generations to come".

The firm, Bulgari, was set up by his grandfather, Greek silversmith Sotirios Voulgaris, in Rome in the late 19th century - who later adapted his name to Bulgari in Italian.

Originally from Paramythia, a mountain village in the Epirus region of northwestern Greece renowned for its silver jewellery, Voulgaris fled Ottoman rule and settled in Naples before opening a shop in Rome.

Paolo Bulgari joined the company shortly after leaving school, becoming an expert in gemstones - eventually leading the business and overseeing international growth.

The Bulgari family favoured bold gemstones, rich yellow gold, generous proportions and unexpected colour combinations to set them apart from the delicate asethetic associated with Parisian jewellery houses.

The business broke into luxury markets and diversified beyond jewellery into watches, perfumes, leather goods and hotels.

"Paolo Bulgari began collaborating with his father in 1960, specialising in the knowledge of precious stones, of which he was one of the world's leading experts, and refining the sensibility and aesthetic sense that are at the root of the Bvlgari style," the Bvlgari company posted on social media., external

"He poured his heart into his craft and into nurturing the talents of young designers, leaving an indelible mark on the world of jewellery.

"Thank you, Mr. Paolo, your legacy as a brilliant beacon of creative inspiration will continue to inspire generations to come."

Paolo Bulgari alongside David Bowie and his wife Iman in September 1991 in Versailles, France.

The post included a quote from Paolo Bulgari: "Everything can be a source of inspiration. There are no rules in creativity. Never."

The designer brand's celebrity clients have included Elizabeth Taylor, Audrey Hepburn, Marilyn Monroe, Ingrid Bergman, Sophie Loren, Anne Hathaway, Zendaya and Rihanna.

Actor Zendaya wearing a Bvlgari necklace at the 2025 Golden Globe awards.

In 2011 the family sold its controlling stake to the French luxury group LVMH, in a deal worth about 3.7 billion euros.

Chairman and CEO of LVMH, Bernard Arnault - who is one of the world's richest people - offered his condolences and also paid tribute to Paolo Bulgari.

"Throughout his life, he was a key architect of the brand's success, while simultaneously upholding and embodying the Italian spirit and the deeply Roman heritage of our exceptional jewellery Maison," he said in a statement to the BBC.

In 2019, Paolo Bulgari and his wife established the Paolo Bulgari Foundation to support young people through schooling and job training in disadvantaged neighbourhoods on the outskirts of Rome.

The funeral will take place in Rome on Monday, 23 November in the Basilica of Santa Croce in Gerusalemme.

#133. 26-year-old Google worker earns $323,000 a year—but lives on less than minimum wage

This story is part of CNBC Make It's Millennial Money series, which details how people around the world earn, spend and save their money.

At 26 years old, Google AI security analyst and YouTuber Charles Broomfield is on track to earn about $323,000 this year — but he lives on less than minimum wage.

For the past few years, Broomfield has kept his annual spending on core living expenses under $20,000, less than the roughly $23,700 a full-time worker would earn at the minimum wage, averaged across all 50 states.

He does it by living with roommates in Washington, D.C., walking or biking instead of driving, eating free meals at work and rarely spending beyond his basic needs. Even when he splurges on something fun, he keeps things modest; in August, that meant a baseball game and a single $7 beer.

"I guess something else that probably does make me a little bit unique is I really don't feel joy when I buy things," he says. "I feel like I have what I need."

There is a goal behind all that saving. Broomfield has pursued financial independence since college and now expects to reach his target of $750,000 in investments within the next year, well ahead of his original goal of doing so by age 30. As his income has climbed, his lifestyle has largely stayed the same, he says.

Here's how Broomfield keeps his spending so low and what he plans to do once he no longer needs the paycheck.

Broomfield grew up in Colorado Springs, Colorado, the son of two military veterans. He and his siblings had to set aside half of everything they earned growing up. They could spend the other half or earn interest from their parents by putting that money away, too.

By his teenage years, Broomfield had become a disciplined saver. At the time, he dreamed of using his cash stash to buy some of the familiar trappings of wealth.

"I wanted a Lamborghini and I wanted some nice penthouse," he says. "I just wanted them because I thought other people wanted them."

At 17, Broomfield discovered Mr. Money Mustache, a personal finance blog by a former software engineer who retired at 30. The blog advocates living frugally and investing much of your income to build a portfolio large enough to support you through withdrawals, without needing a paycheck. It introduced Broomfield to the financial independence, retire early — or FIRE — movement and gave all that saving a new purpose.

"All I had to do was live super frugally and I could actually reach financial freedom by the time I was 30 and go do whatever actually made me happy in life," he says.

The idea stuck with him. After graduating from Knox College in 2022, Broomfield moved to Northern Virginia for a job paying more than $100,000 a year and set a goal of reaching $750,000 in investments before 30 while spending less than $25,000 a year.

He also decided to start at $0. Broomfield had roughly $50,000 in savings when he graduated, with his parents having covered the portion of his college tuition not paid for by scholarships. He wanted his FIRE total to reflect only what he earned after college.

In his first year after college, Broomfield was "super geeked out" about reaching financial freedom, he says.

"It's almost the only thing I thought about, because I was building all these new habits and making real money for really the first time in my life," he says.

That enthusiasm sometimes took him to extremes. He even considered living in a van parked in his employer's garage to avoid paying rent.

"I crunched the numbers on it, and effectively every year that I spent living in a van rather than paying rent would allow me to retire approximately one month faster," he says. "It just [didn't] seem really worth it to go to that extreme."

But Broomfield still found satisfaction in squeezing every dollar out of his budget. He once biked two miles to a grocery store to save $1 on toothpaste rather than buy it at the Whole Foods next to his apartment.

"For a lot of people, they feel their wins by buying stuff for themselves," Broomfield says. "I get my wins by seeing how much I can invest."

Looking back, Broomfield acknowledges that some of his early efforts to save money made little difference in how quickly he could reach financial independence.

"I never expect anybody else to do exactly what I'm doing," he says. "I know I'm way more of a geek about this than most people, and I'm particularly well positioned to be able to live this kind of life."

Broomfield's core living expenses totaled $1,477 in August, excluding savings, investments and payments toward his graduate school tuition.

Here's a breakdown of where his money went that month, including those additional payments:

Roughly 90% of Broomfield's income went to savings and investments in August, a fairly typical month for him, he says. He put about $7,931 into his 401(k) and another $10,000 into a taxable brokerage account.

Another $700 went toward a 0% interest employer tuition repayment plan with a former employer, leaving him with a remaining balance of $16,800 in August.

Housing is Broomfield's biggest expense, though he keeps costs down by sharing an apartment with four roommates. In a neighborhood where one-bedroom apartments commonly rent for more than $2,000, the arrangement saves him about $1,000 a month.

One thing Broomfield is willing to splurge on is a martial arts gym membership. He pays $229 a month to train as a kickboxer, even though he could work out for free at Google's office gym. Broomfield considers it a "young person's sport" and doesn't want to postpone competing until after he reaches financial independence. It's also a way to socialize with other amateur fighters who share his interest.

Broomfield doesn't own a car and generally walks, bikes or takes public transportation. Google covers his daily Metro commute, which helps keep his transportation costs low. He tries to meet friends near Metro stations and, when visiting his grandfather in Virginia, takes public transit as far as he can before using Uber for the last leg of the trip.

Before joining Google, Broomfield typically spent around $200 a month on food. Now that he eats most of his meals at work, he spends about $70 a month on average. His $28 food bill in August was especially low because he had ingredients left over from July.

He also cooks less at home these days. Previously, he relied more on batch cooking, including pressure cooker chili that makes nine servings for about $2 each. For snacks, he might have a bowl of Raisin Bran or celery with peanut butter.

“I did the math once, and peanut butter was the lowest cost thing per gram of protein in the entire grocery store,” he says in a video on his YouTube channel, which he started in 2023 to document his FIRE journey.

For entertainment, he relies on the free version of YouTube, where he watches educational content about startups, business and entrepreneurship, or listens to music.

Broomfield expects to make about $323,000 in 2026, more than triple the roughly $100,000 he earned in his first year out of college. His compensation rose to about $120,000 over his first three years, then jumped to $200,000 when he joined Amazon Web Services in 2025. After a brief stint at Capital One, he moved to Google this year. By keeping his expenses low, investing most of what he earns and riding an upward-trending stock market, he has built up about $650,000 in investments since graduating from college.

Broomfield earns money from his YouTube channel as well, which has expanded to offer personal finance and career advice. About $48,000 of his projected income this year will come from the channel and a related career-coaching business.

Even as his income has grown, Broomfield has stuck with roommates, public transportation and the same modest spending habits.

"I've really separated how happy I am from how much money I spend," he says. "I truly don't believe that I would be significantly happier if I spent a lot more money."

Much of the money he doesn't spend goes straight into investments. He maxes out tax-advantaged accounts including his 401(k) and HSA, and has sometimes contributed 100% of his paycheck to his 401(k) early in the year to reach the annual limit as quickly as possible.

Broomfield keeps his investment strategy simple, favoring broad-market index funds.

"I don't try and pick individual stocks. I just don't have the time to do the research and understand the risk and potential returns of those individual stocks," he says. "And the research shows that most people who try to pick individual stocks don't end up doing better than if they just invested in the stock market as a whole."

He avoids cryptocurrency for a similar reason. Broomfield says he doesn't understand what gives it value and doesn't want to put money into investments he doesn't understand.

At his current pace, Broomfield expects to reach his $750,000 investment goal within the next year.

When Broomfield discovered the FIRE movement at 17, he worried about being stuck in what he imagined as a "soul-crushing" 9-to-5 office job until age 65. He saw financial independence as a way to quit working altogether.

But while financial independence would give him enough money to live without a paycheck, Broomfield no longer sees early retirement as the goal.

“Once I reach financial freedom, maybe this is a little ironic, but I actually think I will keep working,” he says.

His YouTube business already earns enough in most months to cover his expenses without a full-time job. With that income and his growing investments, Broomfield hopes to eventually build a business of his own, potentially in AI security.

"Having complete ownership and complete buy-in to something that's my own. That sounds really exciting to me," he says.

Whatever he chooses to do, Broomfield sees financial independence as a way to keep his options open. He bases his savings goal on the so-called “4% rule, a retirement guideline that suggests withdrawing 4% of your investments in the first year, then adjusting withdrawals for inflation.

For Broomfield, that would mean roughly $30,000 a year from a $750,000 portfolio. That's enough to cover his current expenses, though he recognizes that could change if he eventually starts a family.

"There's a very good chance I won't want to live the way I do forever. That's one of the reasons I'm doing it now. I'm young. I'm healthy. I'm single," he says. "I totally understand that somebody with a family probably doesn't want to live with a bunch of roommates."

But the money Broomfield has already saved gives him more freedom to choose where and how much he works without feeling tied to a paycheck.

"Having that peace of mind to know that I'll be fine, even if I can't find work quickly or even if I get laid off tomorrow, that has just been a huge breath of fresh air for myself," he says.

#132. Can AI really help spot Black Friday deals? We put it to the test

Whether it's scrolling through endless choice, spotting fake reviews, or working out if a "deal" is a deal at all, Black Friday sums up a lot of online woes.

Retailers promise bargains - but the sheer number of offers can leave us feeling no closer to finding the best deal.

It's an area where generative AI products, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini, have promised to help.

But are they any good at finding Black Friday deals? We had a go ourselves a week ahead of the big day.

Figures suggest relatively few UK shoppers currently go to AI tools for help.

A recent YouGov survey of 19 countries found the UK ranked second-last in the proportion of people who said they had used AI to help them shop.

Crucially, 36% of respondents who said they would be shopping around Black Friday didn't plan to use an AI tool at all.

But the survey suggested a growing number of people were considering it - and tech firms have released features to capitalise on this.

Amazon launched Alexa for Shopping for UK users in November and OpenAI launched a Shopping Research tool on ChatGPT days before last year's Black Friday.

In the US, Google recently launched Universal Cart, with AI-powered features such as price tracking and identification of relevant promo codes.

But there has been criticism that fact-checking AI ends up taking more time, while some brands are working to try to make sure their products show up in chatbot answers.

We asked four models to find a 55-inch TV deal, using the free versions of many of the most popular tools.

While this was not a rigorous scientific test, the experience we had produced mixed results.

OpenAI's ChatGPT came up with four options.

Its top pick was a Hisense TV on Chinese retailer Joybuy's website, on sale for Â£499 down from Â£649.

The next recommendation was a 55-inch TCL-branded TV, but a sidebar showed prices for the 50-inch version, from Â£379 to Â£814.

Option three was the same Hisense TV as before, but for full price at Â£649 from John Lewis and Richer Sounds.

This was the "best safer purchase" according to ChatGPT, as the sites offered longer guarantee periods.

But clicking on the links did not go well. Both retailers said the TV was no longer listed online.

The fourth option was equally fruitless - another TCL TV, at full price, Â£150 over our budget and not available online.

At the bottom, ChatGPT served one last offer - "one OLED option to compare" - for a Â£1,100 LG TV that was marked as "Ad".

Claude produced the shortest answer of all the models we tested, and unlike the others, it did not give much detail on the specifications of each model.

Instead, it seemed to base its results on a small number of reviews published online.

It recommended the same two TCL TVs as ChatGPT.

But when we clicked on the links, they sent us to two dead pages on the Richer Sounds website.

Some other links led to year-old round-ups of Black Friday 2025 deals.

Claude was not very enthusiastic about its other two recommendations due to a lack of independent reviews - calling one "a decent option" but saying it "wouldn't pick" the other.

It also listed two Samsung TVs, but it told us not to buy them, with one "over budget and not clearly better than the TCLs".

We were left wondering why it even mentioned them in the first place.

Different AI model, same TV manufacturers - albeit different Hisense and TCL models to the previous chatbots' recommendations.

Gemini generated a table comparing four different models, but offered no links to the stores it named, so we searched for them ourselves.

The prices for one TCL TV were correct at Â£649, but it was sold out at one of the named retailers.

It said the other TCL model was available from Argos and Currys for Â£539 - correct for Argos, but when we checked Currys, it was Â£60 more expensive.

Again, it included two Samsung models to avoid due to lower screen specs.

Alexa came back with four TVs, from its own Amazon Ember at Â£532 to a Â£699 Samsung.

But its top recommendation was to go for another Samsung model at Â£616 (down from Â£749), based on its picture quality and Amazon customer rating.

Its fourth option was for a Sony TV, but it was only 50 inches, leaving us unsure why it had included it.

All the prices stated were correct, but Amazon only searches its own store. Alexa did acknowledge this, encouraging us to check its rivals for better deals, "as prices can vary" and we could get longer guarantees elsewhere.

There are methods that can boost the likelihood of getting a better response, from crafting more informative prompts to upping a chatbot's "thinking" level.

Tech journalist Alex Hughes had better luck than we did when he used ChatGPT's agentic AI mode to find a TV in last year's Black Friday sales.

Agentic AI is still in its early days, but it allows AI to act with more autonomy and carry out multi-step tasks.

The search took about 20 minutes "with very little work on my end needed," Hughes wrote for Tom's Guide, external in November 2025.

A year on, he told the BBC he didn't go through with buying a TV at that time.

He did later purchase one of the seven models ChatGPT had surfaced - but he had found it for a lower price in a bricks-and-mortar shop.

Will he be using AI to find Black Friday deals again this year? "Probably not," he said.

He reckoned it saved him time, but "when you're trying to deal with something like live price data... it will be inconsistent and quite often incorrect".

While we were served up the same brands again and again, in reality there are tonnes of products listed for sale around Black Friday.

Naureen Mohammed, who advises businesses on how to get their products to appear in AI results, told the BBC customers did not see the full picture when they talked to chatbots.

If businesses fail to update their prices when people start hunting for bargains, "last year's prices are served up as today's deals".

"I suspect the whole model for advertising is going to start influencing AI recommendations," she said.

Her own analysis of chatbots' Black Friday tech deals in the UK suggested the big retailers came out on top - with Amazon mentioned in half of responses, and Currys following closely behind.

Her tips include asking an assistant "is that this year's price?" and clicking on the links provided - "because you shouldn't take any advice from the AI system at face value".

But consumer group Which? recently said inaccurate pricing in AI results added more work, external to Black Friday deal-hunting.

"If you ask ChatGPT for a list of 10 laptops under Â£400 and seven of the results are priced higher, you've likely wasted a good chunk of time going through them to discover the bot gave you only three options," said senior researcher Hannah Walsh.

"The opposite is also true - you could miss out on great models in your budget if ChatGPT tells you they are more expensive than they actually are."

Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

#130. Walmart hikes outlook as CEO tells CNBC retailer aims to keep prices low despite soaring fuel costs

Walmart on Thursday reported earnings and revenue that narrowly topped estimates and raised its full-year outlook as it saw strong e-commerce growth ahead of the busy holiday season.

Walmart said global e-commerce sales surged 17% in the fiscal third quarter, driven by store-fulfilled pickup and delivery, with Walmart U.S. up 23% and Sam's Club U.S. rising 30%. Its Walmart U.S. advertising business saw a 37% sales increase for the quarter.

Walmart said it rolled back prices on nearly 8,000 products across its stores, a focus as U.S. consumers worry about the cost of living in the face of elevated food and gas prices.

CEO John Furner told CNBC's Becky Quick that the company has seen higher-than-expected diesel costs, but that tariff refunds have helped the retailer to offset those inputs and maintain lower prices.

"The reality is that increases in diesel fuel and transportation, they do work their way into cost of goods," Furner said in an exclusive interview. "So we're hoping to see these costs come down as soon as possible, and we'll keep prices as low as we can."

CFO John David Rainey told CNBC that growth in online orders for Walmart and Sam's Club was driven by an emphasis on speed of delivery and reduced prices. He added that Walmart saw its largest share gains in the food category in three years during the third quarter.

"This was another really solid quarter for us, we feel good about it," Rainey told CNBC. "We continue to gain share while continuing to improve the profitability of our digital businesses."

Walmart also raised its full-year guidance, now expecting net sales to increase between 4.9% and 5.2%, compared with previous guidance of a 4% to 5% increase. Rainey said Walmart is maintaining "flexibility" in its guidance.

Walmart anticipates adjusted earnings per share will come in between $2.84 and $2.89, compared with a prior range of $2.80 to $2.87. It's also now expecting adjusted operating income to increase between 8% and 8.6%, up from the previous outlook of between 7% and 8.5% growth.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, the company reported net income of $4.95 billion, or 62 cents per share, down from $6.14 billion, or 77 cents per share, a year earlier. Revenue rose 5.2%.

Adjusting for investment gains and losses, tax matters and reorganization charges, Walmart earned 65 cents per share.

Walmart U.S. recorded net sales of $125 billion, up 3.6% from a year earlier. Its comparable sales excluding fuel rose 2.9%, in line with Wall Street expectations, according to StreetAccount.

Furner also said Walmart is seeing encouraging adoption of its artificial intelligence agent, named Sparky, especially ahead of the holiday season. He said it's led to customer basket increases of 40% on average.

"It just is a great way of just completing the entire basket and solving entire the solution for people," he said.

Sam's Club reported net sales of $25.9 billion, marking a 10% increase from the year-ago period. It said its sales were driven by strength in the grocery category and general merchandise.

In October, Walmart announced it was launching a joint membership option with Walmart+ and Sam's Club as it leans further into convenience. Both stores will still offer stand-alone memberships, as well.

Furner told CNBC the new membership has brought roughly 2 million new people into Sam's Club since its launch in late October. Though the company reimbursed some members for the new membership rate, Rainey added that it's already seeing positive returns on the joint model and that consumers are "spending appreciably more."

The company on Thursday said its global membership fee revenue grew 17% in the third quarter.

The company is typically well insulated from macroeconomic pressures because of its value reputation. Last quarter, the company said it was lowering prices of products across multiple categories, including beef.

Rainey said Thursday that he's "optimistic" about consumer spending heading into the holiday season and that the fourth quarter is already "off to a pretty good start."

The big-box retailer has posted solid growth over the past few quarters as it sees customers gravitate to reduced prices, improvements in delivery and gains in its advertising business. It's also leaned into attracting higher-income consumers with its Walmart+ membership.

Shares of Walmart have fluctuated this year but are up roughly 13% year to date.

#129. Quantum computing’s first real-world payoff may come in energy, not drug discovery

For years, the case for quantum computing has rested on ambitious promises, from discovering new drugs by simulating how molecules behave to optimizing supply chains and airline schedules. Many of the biggest aims are still likely to be years, if not decades, away.

But quantum software, which uses algorithms designed to solve complex problems faster than conventional computers, is already being tested on a more immediate challenge: the energy transition, from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production.

Phasecraft, a quantum software company founded in Bristol, England, in 2019, is one of the firms putting the technology to work in energy. It raised $34 million in Series B funding in 2025, and in September this year announced plans to set up U.S. headquarters in Arlington, Virginia.

There are enormous opportunities to solve problems that are both important for energy security and commercially valuable, Ashley Montanaro, co-founder and CEO of Phasecraft, told CNBC.

Phasecraft isn't alone in applying quantum software to the energy industry.

Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.

Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently

In a separate project, ORCA worked with Japanese software company JIJ on a challenge power companies face: deciding which generators to switch on or off, and when, to meet demand at the lowest cost.

That project also involved BP and the National Quantum Computing Centre (NQCC), the U.K.'s national laboratory for quantum computing, which is part of the government-backed U.K. Research and Innovation. The involvement of the NQCC, like that of ARPA-E, suggests governments are paying close attention to quantum software's potential role in energy, as are companies such as BP.

For Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator (NESO), the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing.

Phasecraft believes its grid software could be particularly sought after in Asia, where electrification is rapidly expanding, increasing the need for grid resilience to balance high and growing demand with the intermittent supply offered by renewables.

The company is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.

Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency–Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen.

Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, Montanaro said the returns were there too.

"When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.

#128. San Francisco is enjoying an AI boom, but worries about a crash are growing

The city of San Francisco is the heart of the AI sector

Lauren Goldboss is busy. The owner of a small San Francisco dog-walking company, she has been hiring staff and buying vans.

Fuelling the growth is her new office dog-walking service Paw Perk, popular with some local AI start-ups.

Her business is an example of how the AI boom in San Francisco is benefiting other sectors.

The city saw an exodus of residents during the pandemic and its downtown was weakened by remote work, but it s now enjoying a rejuvenation, with AI being the major driver.

Lauren Goldboss walks dogs for AI workers

Leading AI firms OpenAI and Anthropic â which have been hiring top engineering talent, often on eye-popping salaries â are based here. Anthropic, the company behind the Claude chatbot, last week listed on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

Thousands of other AI start-ups have also sprung up, external in San Francisco.

Signs of the boom are evident in soaring rents and house prices, falling office vacancies, and buses and billboards plastered with cryptic AI adverts.

But what if there is a significant downward correction in AI share prices and a resulting big fall in investment in the industry? The International Monetary Fund last month became the latest organization to warn, external that this was a possibility.

Concerns have since continued due to the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang, the former boss of AI firm Nvidia, based south of San Francisco, causing some wider AI stock falls. However, Nvidia remains highly profitable, and Wednesday of this week it reported that its latest quarterly profits had doubled from a year earlier to $65bn (Â£49bn).

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Wealthy AI workers send San Francisco house prices soaring

Follow the money: Who pays for the AI boom now?

What are the thoughts of AI workers in the city, and should San Francisco â no stranger to tech's boom-and-bust cycles â be preparing for a possible crash?

One AI worker, employed by one of the largest companies in the city, is convinced that a crash is coming. "I am certain that the house of cards will come downâ¦ and I'm sure it will affect me," says the man who asked that we didn't use his name.

He questions whether leading US AI companies can generate enough revenues to justify their enormous spending commitments as cheaper, increasingly capable AI models emerge, especially from China.

But he isn't losing sleep over it. "Why worry about things I can't control?"

Another San Francisco-based AI worker, who also asked to remain anonymous, says that a significant market correction is "totally possible".

Yet he adds that most people don't think of it as the core risk. Instead, he says that the bigger concern in his circles, and the one he shares, is the existential risk that AI could pose to humanity.

"In some weird way, a market correction being the worst-case outcome feels great."

If there is an AI crash, San Francisco could be hit far more than Silicon Valley which lies 40 miles to its south.

That's because the AI boom is very much centred on the city itself rather than the wider San Francisco Bay Area, which includes Silicon Valley.

One study, external found that 60% of all venture capital invested in US AI firms from 2023 to 2025 went to the companies located in San Francisco, dwarfing the significantly smaller percentage given to companies in Silicon Valley.

But some factors suggest that the impact of a crash in San Francisco may be softer than some fear.

For all the investment they have attracted, AI companies are employing relatively few workers in the city compared to previous tech booms. So there may not be that many jobs to lose in a downturn.

"Hiring isn't necessarily on the scale as we would have thought with the amount of venture capital and new company formation," says Jeff Bellisario, executive director of the Bay Area Council Economic Institute, a business-affiliated think tank.

Ted Egan, the chief economist for the City and County of San Francisco, adds that "because of AI, it is a good time for tech again in the city, but we are not seeing tonnes of overall tech job growth".

Despite the expansion of the AI sector, the city remains about 35,000 jobs shy of its pre-pandemic peak, estimates Enrico Moretti, an economics professor at the University of California, Berkeley.

Instead, much of the spending by AI firms in the US is going towards data centres and computing power, relatively little of which are located in the Bay Area. San Francisco might be the brains of the AI boom, but it isn't the brawn.

Nonetheless, a significant correction could still be "very painful" for San Francisco, warns Moretti. Less AI hiring and potential layoffs would ripple through the local service sector, likely to affect jobs in everything from restaurants, to retail, to personal services like dog walking.

"It is the spillover on service jobs that would actually be the main effect," he says.

Office vacancies could also rise, reducing downtown foot traffic, while rents and house prices could weaken.

Huge data centres are being built across the US to power AI

But AI isn't the only force driving San Francisco's recovery. Healthcare and tourism â which the city has been working to encourage, including by improving downtown safety and cleanliness â are also growing,, external which may help cushion against a possible AI downturn.

"Tourism is not fully back, but the growth has been healthy," says Egan, adding that it is a sign of confidence in San Francisco.

All told, Egan doesn't expect an AI crash to fundamentally alter San Francisco's economic trajectory.

"The city's economy has not been screaming during this AI boom, so I wouldn't expect it to grind to a halt if there was a slowdown in AI spending," he says. "I think of it primarily as a hiccup that we should expect, rather than a major course correction for the city's economy."

As to whether and how San Francisco's city government should prepare, there are limits to what it can do, note Bellisario and Moretti. But encouraging growth in sectors beyond technology and maintaining financial reserves could help.

For Goldboss, the dog-walking company owner, her philosophy is to "build while it is here". If the next tech bust comes â "always a reality in San Francisco" â she will try to adapt to what the market needs. "It is just the risk you kind of have to take," she says.

#124. Employers are being asked to give interview feedback - here's what applicants want

Ffion, Yugraj and Jessica say being ghosted has become a common part of applying for jobs

Have you ever applied for a job or attended an interview, only to hear absolutely nothing back from the employer?

So-called "job ghosting" has become a big problem for jobseekers in the UK, leaving them "demoralised", denting their confidence and undermining their hopes of finding work.

It's an issue that was mentioned in former minister Alan Milburn's major review into young people who are not in employment, education or training - those dubbed as Neets - this week.

Among his recommendations - aimed at reducing the proportion of Neets from 13% to about 5% by 2036 - Milburn proposed that large employers should give all unsuccessful job applicants under 25 "meaningful feedback".

The report's proposals relate mostly to England, with a recommendation that Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland adapt the principles to suit their own systems.

But is feedback for all applicants realistic, given the vast number of applications these firms receive? And what can people do to improve their chances in such a tough job market?

Yugraj Samrai, 23, from Coventry is currently unemployed and says he has submitted hundreds of job applications since he graduated from his marketing degree in July.

But 30 to 40% of the time he hears nothing back at all, which can make him "feel like a statistic, not a person".

He tells the BBC: "I understand there are very few graduate jobs available, but at least give me a response to say whether I'll progress onto the next stage or not."

Ffion Johns, from Llanelli, has been unemployed since June and did not get an interview until September after submitting 70 applications.

The 24-year-old says her biggest problem throughout the job-hunting process has been the lack of feedback about why she didn't get the job, so she knows what to fix next time.

Sometimes she has even been ghosted after attending an interview.

"With most of these roles I'm not getting a rejection. I'm just not hearing anything, which can be quite frustrating," she says.

Research by head-hunting firm Robert Walters last year found 86% of UK job hunters said their applications had been ignored, with no response at all from the hiring organisation.

The Milburn report, external says this "wall of silence" is especially hard on young people as it gives them "nothing to learn from". It adds that "time and emotional investment" goes into their applications.

"It can really take hours and hours, so it just feels even worse to not even have that be recognised," says Jessica Anderson, 21, from Swindon.

Employers say they are being flooded with job applications in a very competitive jobs market and it can be hard to respond to all of them.

And AI is adding to the problem, enabling applicants to submit large numbers of applications in the hope of improving their chances.

But Meg Burton, a career coach based in Leeds, says firms should at the very least inform job applicants when they are unsuccessful and that AI could help achieve this.

Candidates should also be given proper feedback if they reach the interview stage, she says.

"It doesn't have to be hugely detailed, it's just some pointers on why they were unsuccessful and what they can learn from that experience going forward," she says.

The government could choose to adopt the Milburn proposals and crack down on job ghosting.

But it raises another question: if candidates began receiving significantly more rejections than before, could this itself have a demoralising effect?

Ellie McCrone, 20, from Northampton understands the concern but thinks hearing back and getting constructive feedback where possible is always best.

Ellie says constructive feedback is always preferable to being ghosted

"If you are applying for hundreds of jobs and never getting anything, I think something is going wrong... You can't fix something unless you know what the problem is."

There are things applicants can do to improve their chances and avoid ghosting, such as tailoring their CV to each job and not submitting "hundreds and hundreds of applications" that all look the same, Burton adds.

Candidates should also never take being ghosted personally, she adds.

"Not hearing back doesn't mean you aren't a great candidate. Put it down to experience and move on."

#123. ‘More than just a game’: How GTA VI’s blockbuster launch fuels a booming content economy

Grand Theft Auto VI has exploded out of the gate after a 13-year wait, drawing millions of buyers while fueling a parallel economy of livestreams, clips and other content built around people watching the game as much as playing it.

GTA VI has delivered a blockbuster debut, with about 19.1 million copies sold — including preorders — by Thursday morning, representing roughly $1.84 billion in sales, according to Sensor Tower. The GTA franchise itself has sold more than 470 million units worldwide.

Take-Two Interactive, which owns the studio behind the game, has not yet disclosed a profit figure for the title.

Ross Gerber, president and CEO of Gerber Kawasaki Wealth & Investment Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that he sees further upside for Take-Two, which his firm holds in its portfolio.

"It's crazy good. I've never seen anything like it," Gerber said, hailing the title as "more than just a game" and pointing to the franchise's potential to expand into film and television.

But that launch is also spawning a second economy around the enormous global hype and demand for GTA VI content.

Viewers watched 125.5 million hours of the title's content in its first 24 hours across platforms including Twitch, YouTube and Kick, according to Streams Charts.

YouTube creator Darren "IShowSpeed" Watkins Jr. led streamers with 4.47 million hours viewed across YouTube and Twitch. Separately, Twitch tracker CastAccord recorded about two million hours watched over roughly 36 hours of streamer Kai Cenat's launch broadcast.

Both creators had built anticipation with trailers for their own GTA VI coverage. As of Nov. 19, IShowSpeed's trailer had drawn 3.7 million YouTube views, while Cenat's had amassed 2.7 million.

According to Forbes, IShowSpeed and Cenat are estimated to earn about $30 million and $8.5 million a year, respectively, from their broader creator businesses.

Rockstar Games, the studio behind the franchise, has also moved to capitalize on the surge in viewer interest, partnering with YouTube on a 24-hour launch broadcast and introducing themed gifts that viewers could send during select streams.

Outside creators and agencies are also trying to capture the surge in demand for Grand Theft Auto content.

Agency Clipped & Shipped is offering a $10,000 prize pool to editors who can drive audiences to clips from Kai Cenat's streams, with qualifying submissions distributed across YouTube Shorts, TikTok, Instagram and X.

A separate campaign offered $1 per 1,000 views for clips of streamer KreekCraft, with individual submissions eligible to earn up to $400.

Those audiences could translate into thousands of dollars in creator revenue.

Using AIR Media-Tech's U.S. YouTube Shorts benchmark, 1.5 million eligible engaged views could generate about $490, while 5.1 million would yield roughly $1,670.

The two Vice City Dispatch videos could generate about $3,840 based on higher benchmark rates for long-form content. The channels' actual earnings could not be independently verified.

Moreover, the content economy is becoming increasingly automated.

On X, users are pitching artificial intelligence-assisted clipping operations that scan livestreams for spikes in chat activity, automatically cut highlights and distribute them across multiple accounts.

One user claimed an operation generated $21,000 overnight from 2.7 million views, equivalent to about $7.78 per 1,000 reported views.

The earnings claims could not be independently verified. X bases payouts on qualified impressions from Premium users, which are not reflected in public view counts.

And appetite for more content — and more revenue — may only be starting.

Wedbush analyst Alicia Reese told CNBC that in-game purchases could generate "years of high-margin recurring revenue" for Take-Two, which she believes investors have yet to fully price in.

She expects the console launch to be followed by a PC release and then an online multiplayer mode, mirroring the rollout of the previous title in the franchise.

Rockstar's launch announcement listed PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X and S, with no timetable for either potential follow-on. GTA VI has also picked up eight nominations for the Game Awards, including game of the year, best game direction, narrative, art direction, score and music, audio design, action/adventure and performance.

#121. Gap names new Athleta CEO, says Old Navy turnaround is seeing signs of progress

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 11% Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."

#120. Pip assessments need 'fundamental overhaul', report to say

The disability minister has been looking into disability benefits for a year for his review, which is to be published on Tuesday

A review into the personal independence payment (Pip) will call for a "fundamental overhaul" of the assessment process to "better recognise people's varying costs and needs", the disability minister has said.

Sir Stephen Timms has been looking into disability benefits for the past year and found the set up was "no longer fit for purpose".

Writing in the Times, external, he said his review, to be published in full on Tuesday, would recommend people with the most severe conditions have a "fast track" process which is "less burdensome".

It comes after a separate review into young people not in education, employment or training (Neets) recommended Pip be scrapped and replaced for under-25s.

Sir Stephen said that while Pip had the "right aims" when it was created, there have been "marked shifts in the nature of disability and ill health" in the 13 years since.

He said there had been "substantial growth in claims for mental health and neurodiverse conditions as well as changes to wider society and the workplace".

"For those with the most severe conditions and end of life care, the review recommends a fast track which is less burdensome, with assessment capacity freed up for other cases," he said.

"Someone with a lifelong or degenerative illness should not need regular reassessments which feel adversarial and unnecessary."

He added: "Disabled people must have the chance to participate fully in everyday life, including in work, and need a more personalised, flexible system that supports them to do so, rather than itself being a barrier."

Disability benefit not fit for purpose, minister leading review says

The Timms review was commissioned after a U-turn from former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer's government on a plan for major benefit reform in an attempt to reduce the welfare bill by Â£5bn a year.

More than 130 MPs, including 120 from Labour, signed an amendment to reject plans to cut disability and sickness-related benefit payments in 2025.

The rebels said they opposed the plans because of the number of people expected to be pushed into relative poverty and a lack of consultation at the time.

Number of people claiming key disability benefit hits record high

Sir Stephen's long-awaited review comes only a week after a former Labour Health Secretary Alan Milburn handed down his proposed reforms to tackle youth unemployment.

The government accepted recommendations from the Milburn review to move those aged 18 to 24 off Pip and the health element of universal credit, and onto an entirely new benefits system.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he thought the "same philosophy" could be applied "right through the age range".

At this stage it is unclear how both of the reviews will complement one another.

The cost of Pip is expected to rise to more than Â£41bn by 2030, up from Â£15bn in 2020. It was being claimed by 4m people in the UK as of 30 April 2026, according to the Department for Work and Pensions.

Though reforming the benefit has frequently been looked at as a way to reduce the Treasury's welfare bill, disability charities have described it as a lifeline for many.

Ahead of the Timms review being handed down, disability equality charity Scope said ministers must ensure Pip is not weakened.

"Life costs more when you're disabled, whether you work or not," executive director James Taylor said, adding that Pip was "vital financial support".

"We know there is real fear among disabled people that changes could leave them unable to afford the essentials."

'Complete system reset' needed to get 440,000 young people into work, says major review

#119. Tata Steel to buy $1.67 billion stake in India’s largest steel pipe maker; shares plunge nearly 5%

Tata Steel, one of the world's leading steel producers, on Monday said it will buy a 26.4% stake in India's largest structural steel pipe and tube maker, APL Apollo, for 155.1 billion rupees ($1.67 billion).

The acquisition will boost Tata Steel's downstream integration to around 53% from 35%, the company said in a stock exchange filing. That will lead to more value addition, improved product mix, and greater exposure to higher-margin downstream segments, it said.

Shares of Tata Steel were nearly 5% lower on Monday, while APL Apollo shares tanked over 10% in early trade.

APL Apollo said after the deal it plans to more than double the downstream capacity to 15.5 million tons from the current 7.5 million tons, making it a leading global downstream steel firm.

This capacity addition will be completed by 2035 and would include around 9 million tons per annum of steel tubes and around 6.5 million tons per annum of color-coated products, as per Tata Steel.

Tata Steel will acquire a stake from existing shareholders of APL Apollo for 2,100 rupees per share and will ​also make an open offer to acquire an additional 26% ‌stake for 2,301.40 rupees a piece.

If the open offer is successful, Tata Steel would get a controlling stake of 52.38% in APL Apollo. World's second-largest steel maker ArcelorMittal was also reportedly in the fray to acquire APL Apollo.

The acquisition of APL Apollo would "create a downstream powerhouse for the future" that will serve the growing Indian market, T V Narendran, chief executive and managing director of Tata Steel, said in the release.

Tata Steel, which has an annual crude steel capacity of over 35 million tons per annum, is one of the world's most geographically diversified steel producers with presence across India, Europe and Thailand.

#117. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.
